(179)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{1\over L_{1}}\ln\left[1+\sum\limits_{q=1}^{N-1}{N-q\over N}\left(e^{i{2\pi\over N}eq}\;{\langle 0|(-)^{F}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}}\hat{T}_{2}^{q}|0\rangle\over\langle 0|(-)^{F}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}}|0\rangle}+e^{-i{2\pi\over N}eq}\;{\langle 0|(-)^{F}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}}\hat{T}_{2}^{-q}|0\rangle\over\langle 0|(-)^{F}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}}|0\rangle}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-{1\over L_{1}}\ln\left[1+\sum\limits_{q=1}^{N-1}{N-q\over N}\left(e^{i{2\pi\over N}eq}\;\Xi_{q}+e^{-i{2\pi\over N}eq}\;\Xi_{-q}\right)\right],

where we defined

\displaystyle\Xi_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\langle 0|(-)^{F}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}}\hat{T}_{2}^{q}|0\rangle\over\langle 0|(-)^{F}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}}|0\rangle}\bigg|_{L_{1}\rightarrow\infty}

(180)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle{\int[{\cal{D}}A{\cal{D}}\lambda]^{A(x_{1}=0)=iT_{2}^{q}dT_{2}^{-q},\;A(x_{1}=+L_{1})=0}_{\{n_{34}=1\}}\;e^{-S_{SYM}-S_{m}}\over\int[{\cal{D}}A{\cal{D}}\lambda]^{A(x_{1}=0)=A(x_{1}=L_{1})=0}_{\{n_{34}=1\}}\;e^{-S_{SYM}-S_{m}}}\bigg|_{L_{1}\rightarrow\infty,L\Lambda N\ll 1}\,.

In the last equation above, we converted the matrix elements to the path integral representation. Upon doing so, in the second line above we dropped the convolution with the wave functionals
\Psi_{0}[A]
of the initial and final state
|0\rangle
(recall the discussion after (
173
)), in effect replacing them with
\delta
-functionals, taking the initial and final values of
A
in the path integral to be equal to the classical values in the
|0\rangle
vacuum. We expect that dropping these factors will have an effect that is absorbed in the—at any rate, incalculable, at present, see below—pre-exponential factor in
\Xi_{q}
. In the numerator of (
180
), we also took into account the fact that the insertion of
\hat{T}_{2}^{q}
twists the boundary condition of the fields at
x_{1}=0
(this implements the
n_{12}=-q
twist). We also indicated that the boundary conditions on the spatial
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
are twisted by
n_{34}=1
.

Let us now make the following comments:

1.

The
\ln\langle 0|(-)^{F}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}}|0\rangle
term appearing on the r.h.s. of (
179
) vanishes in SYM but not in SYM
∗
. While it can receive semiclassical corrections from integer-
Q
sectors, including perturbative corrections proportional to
|m|^{2}
, the point is that it does not depend on
e
and does not affect the energy splittings between the
N
flux sectors.

2.

It is clear that the numerator of
\Xi_{q}
, eqn. (
180
), receives contributions from topological sectors with
Q={q\over N}+{\mathbb{Z}}
, while the denominator receives perturbative contributions plus contributions of sectors of arbitrary integer topological charge, including zero.

In this regard, we recall that the SYM
∗
partition function
Z^{T}
defined in (
3
), ignoring the issue of regularization, can be written as a sum over the numerators of
\Xi_{q}
from (
180
):

\displaystyle Z^{T}=\sum_{q=0}^{N-1}\int[{\cal{D}}A{\cal{D}}\lambda]^{A(x_{1}=0)=iT_{2}^{q}dT_{2}^{-q},\;A(x_{1}=+L_{1})=0}_{\{n_{34}=1\}}\;e^{-S_{SYM*}+i\theta Q}\,.

(181)

Now, in the small
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
limit (
L\Lambda N\ll 1
) one expects to be able to semiclassically evaluate the path integrals
\Xi_{q}
contributing to the electric flux energies (
179
).
However, the
L_{1}\gg L
asymmetric limit of a four-torus is not one where the saddle points of topological charge
{q\over N}+{\mathbb{Z}}
are known analytically.
There is evidence, based on minimizing the lattice action (for
SU(2)
and
SU(3)
) on
{\mathbb{T}}^{4}
with twists
n_{34}=1,n_{12}=-q
that in the limit of an asymmetric
{\mathbb{T}}^{4}
, in topological sectors with
Q=q/N+{\mathbb{Z}}
, the saddle points can be represented as a gas of charge
1/N
objects, as we now describe.
55
55

55

Independent of the lattice evidence, one can show, both on
{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{3}_{n_{34}}
and
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{n_{34}}
, with
nonzero twist
n_{34}
,
in either
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
or
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
(as indicated by subscript), that finite-action Euclidean solutions on
{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{3}_{n_{34}}
and
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{n_{34}}
fall into topological classes labelled by fractional topological charges
Q={p\over N^{\prime}}+n
, with
p=0,...,N^{\prime}-1
and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, with
N^{\prime}\equiv{N\over{\rm{gcd}}(n_{34},N)}
. Thus, the numerical solution shown on figure
1
becomes a minimum action solution on
{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{3}_{n_{34}}
(for additional study of the “infinite”-time limit of the lattice solution, see
Wandler:2024hsq
).

Each such object occupies space-time volume
L^{4}
and has topological charge
1/N
. We refer to figure
1
for a picture of the action density of the
Q=1/2
solution in
SU(2)
YM theory obtained on the lattice, which illustrates the nature of these
Q=1/N
solutions in the
L_{1}\gg L
limit. Based on this evidence,
56
56

56

Further support is also provided by the recent analytical treatment of
Hayashi:2024yjc
;
Guvendik:2024umd
;
Hayashi:2024psa
;
Hayashi:2025doq
, utilizing an asymmetric limit of
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
with twist.
one can proceed to evaluate
\Xi_{q}
using a dilute gas approximation, where these charge
1/N
objects are the fundamental “constituents” saturating the path integral. Each of these objects, centered at
x_{1}^{*}
in the time coordinate, interpolates between values of the Wilson loop
W_{2}=e^{i{2\pi\over N}k}
at
x_{1}-x_{1}^{*}\ll-L
to
W_{2}=e^{i{2\pi\over N}(k+1)}
at
x_{1}-x_{1}^{*}\gg L
, i.e., recalling (
174
), between two neighboring
k
-vacua.

The other feature used in argument for the dilute gas approximation, the splitting of a
Q=q/N
instanton into
q

Q=1/N
localized objects in the small-
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
, large-time limit, can be seen, e.g. in figure 11 of
Anber:2025yub
(for
N=3
,
q=2
the dissociation can be seen already on an asymmetric lattice of size
(32,4,12,12)
).

3.

Before we continue, we stress that evaluating the path integrals appearing in (
180
,
179
) by a dilute gas of the charge-
1/N
objects described above is an assumption, as far as sectors with
q>1
is concerned.

Figure 1:
The action density of a charge-
1/2
instanton in
SU(2)
YM theory on an asymmetric
{\mathbb{T}}^{4}
with
L_{1}\gg L
(
L_{1}=48,L=12
) on the lattice with a unit twist in the small
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
and a unit twist in the mixed space-time direction. The plots are taken from
Wandler:2024hsq
. Notice the different axes labeling on the plot, namely
x_{0}^{plot}\rightarrow x_{1}
,
x_{3}^{plot}\rightarrow x_{2}
,
x_{2}^{plot}\rightarrow x_{3}
,
x_{1}^{plot}\rightarrow x_{4}
(thus,
W_{3}
on the plot should be understood as
W_{2}
).
The points the plot illustrates are:
i.)
that the action is localized in a region of size
L
and spacetime volume
L^{4}
and
ii.)
that the solution interpolates between the two
|k\rangle
vacua (
173
,
174
) of the
SU(2)
theory, the
|k=0\rangle
,
W_{2}=1
, and the
|k=1\rangle
,
W_{2}=-1
, as described in the text.
The dilute gas picture advocated in
RTN:1993ilw
;
Gonzalez-Arroyo:1995ynx
uses these instantons as the basic blocks. Thus, in the
L_{1}\rightarrow\infty
limit, one sums over arbitrary even numbers of these instantons, if the boundary conditions in the path integral are untwisted, i.e. if one is computing a transition amplitude
|0\rangle\rightarrow|0\rangle
(or
|1\rangle\rightarrow|1\rangle
). On the other hand, if the transition amplitude is twisted, as it would be in the numerator of
\Xi_{1}
of eqn. (
183
), and interpolates from
|0\rangle\rightarrow|1\rangle
(or v.v.), one sums over an arbitrary odd number of such instantons. This picture, adapted here to
SU(N)
, leads to eqn. (
186
) for the splitting of the energies of the electric flux sectors.

To recapitulate, our final expression for the minimum energy in each
e_{2}=e
flux sector is:

\displaystyle E_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{E}-{1\over L_{1}}\ln\left[1+\sum\limits_{q=1}^{N-1}{N-q\over N}\left(e^{i{2\pi\over N}eq}\;\Xi_{q}+e^{-i{2\pi\over N}eq}\;\Xi_{-q}\right)\right],~e=0,\ldots N-1,

where
\bar{E}
denotes the
e
-independent piece in (
179
) and
\Xi_{q}
is defined in (
180
), from which we reproduce only the path integral expression:

\displaystyle\Xi_{q}\simeq{\int[{\cal{D}}A{\cal{D}}\lambda]^{A(x_{1}=0)=iT_{2}^{q}dT_{2}^{-q},\;A(x_{1}=+L_{1})=0}_{\{n_{34}=1\}}\;e^{-S_{SYM}-S_{m}}\over\int[{\cal{D}}A{\cal{D}}\lambda]^{A(x_{1}=0)=A(x_{1}=L_{1})=0}_{\{n_{34}=1\}}\;e^{-S_{SYM}-S_{m}}}\bigg|_{L_{1}\rightarrow\infty,L\Lambda\ll 1}.

(183)

We now assume that the basic instantons have charge
\pm{1\over N}
and fugacity given by

\displaystyle\zeta L^{-4}\;e^{\pm i{\theta\over N}}\;e^{-{8\pi^{2}\over g^{2}N}}=\zeta L^{-4}\;e^{\pm i{\theta\over N}}\;e^{-S_{0}},~\text{where}~S_{0}\equiv{8\pi^{2}\over g^{2}N},

(184)

where
\zeta
is an unknown dimensionless coefficient, including
g^{2}\equiv g^{2}(L)
dependence, and we have introduced the notation
S_{0}
for the action of a charge-
1/N
instanton.
Thus, we have that (taking
m
real and small) the leading contribution to
E_{e}
of (
) is given by
57
57

57

Recall that all
\Xi_{q}
vanish if
m=0
, keeping all fluxes degenerate.

\displaystyle e^{i{2\pi e\over N}}\Xi_{1}+e^{-i{2\pi e\over N}}\Xi_{-1}=\zeta{L_{1}L^{3}\over L^{4}}\;mL\cos\left({2\pi e+\theta\over N}\right)e^{-S_{0}}(1+{\cal{O}}(e^{-NS_{0}}))~.

(185)

The equation inside the logarithm in (
) has to exponentiate after proper evaluation which we will not attempt here,
58
58

58

A more detailed semiclassical evaluation of (
), including higher orders, would be an exercise of interest, for, among others, resurgence theory; see
Dunne:2016nmc
for review.
hence, we simply expand the logarithm, keeping the above contribution only, to obtain
59
59

59

One notes that, at
\theta=\pi
, there is a two fold degeneracy of flux states,
E_{e}=E_{N-1-e}
, consistent with the parity-center anomaly of SYM
∗
for real
m
(for even-
N
, all flux states, while for odd-
N
, all states with
e\neq{N-1\over 2}
, are doubly degenerate). We also stress that the consistency of (
186
) with the center-parity anomaly is not an artifact of our keeping the
\Xi_{1}
term only: all other terms in (
) depend on
e
only in the combination

e^{iq{2\pi e+\theta\over N}}
, implying that
E_{e}
at
\theta=\pi
are invariant under
e\leftrightarrow N-1-e
(thus interchanging
\Xi_{q}
and
\Xi_{-q}
).

\displaystyle E_{e}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\bar{E}-\zeta^{\prime}me^{-S_{0}}\cos\left({2\pi e+\theta\over N}\right)+\ldots,~e=0,\ldots N-1.

(186)

This equation is a desirable outcome: it shows that the minimum energies in each electric flux sector are split at order
m
. Unfortunately, an analytic calculation of the coefficient
\zeta^{\prime}
is currently out of reach, as already alluded to in the title of this section. We also note that an equation identical to (
186
) can be obtained by soft-supersymmetry breaking methods directly on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, for example as in
Konishi:1996iz
.

Relation to older studies on
{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
:
Let us now comment on the relation between our analysis of SYM
∗
on
{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
and older studies of pure YM theory in the literature
RTN:1993ilw
;
Gonzalez-Arroyo:1995ynx
, which may be not as familiar as they deserve to be.

1.
The splitting of the perturbatively degenerate electric flux energies in
SU(2)
pure YM theory on
{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
at
\theta=0
due to nonperturbative effects was studied a long time ago
RTN:1993ilw
using lattice simulations and semiclassical ideas. The semiclassical expression obtained in a dilute gas approximation was fitted to lattice data to determine the incalculable pre-exponential factor
\zeta^{\prime}
. Adapted to our notation and our choice of
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
twist, their result is obtained from (
186
), by taking
N=2
and
\theta=0
:
E_{1}-E_{0}=2\zeta^{\prime}me^{-S_{0}}
. Their fitting parameter was, in effect,
\zeta^{\prime}m
—we stress that SYM
∗
is in the universality class of pure YM, hence the fact that the equations are related shouldn’t come as surprise. In addition, the
L
-dependence of the energy split, obtained by replacing
e^{-S_{0}}\sim(\Lambda L)^{11\over 3}
appears to fit well the lattice data, as shown in
RTN:1993ilw
;
Gonzalez-Arroyo:1995ynx
.

For completeness, let us also describe how electric flux differences are measured on the lattice
RTN:1993ilw
;
Gonzalez-Arroyo:1995ynx
(here, we generalize their discussion to
SU(N)
and note that the discussion for SYM
∗
is identical to that for pure YM). To disentangle the energetics in the softly broken theory,
consider now the following correlator in the SYM
∗
theory:

\displaystyle\langle(W_{2}^{\dagger}(x_{1}=T))^{k}(W_{2}(x_{1}=0))^{k}\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\,(-1)^{F}e^{-{(L_{1}-T)}H}(W_{2}^{\dagger}(0))^{k}e^{-TH}(W_{2}(0)^{k})\over\mathrm{Tr}\,(-1)^{F}e^{-L_{1}H}

in the limit
L_{1}-T\rightarrow\infty
,
T\rightarrow\infty
. In this limit, only the lowest energy states contribute to the correlator. We insert two complete set of states, schematically
1\equiv\sum\limits_{E,e_{2}}|E,e_{2}\rangle\langle E,e_{2}|
in the numerator of (
6
). To proceed, let us denote
|E_{0},0\rangle
the
lowest energy state in the SYM
∗
theory, assuming that it corresponds to vanishing
e_{2}=0
. The operator
W_{2}^{k}
changes the flux by
k
units. Let us also denote the lowest energy state in the
e_{2}=k
sector by
|E_{k},k\rangle
. Thus, in the large
L_{1}
,
T
limits, we obtain

\displaystyle\langle(W_{2}^{\dagger}(x_{1}=T))^{k}(W_{2}(x_{1}=0))^{k}\rangle\bigg|_{L_{1},T,L_{1}-T\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{e^{-E_{0}(L_{1}-T)}e^{-TE_{k}}|\langle E_{0},0|(W_{2}^{\dagger})^{k}|E_{k},k\rangle|^{2}\over e^{-E_{0}L_{1}}}

(188)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-T(E_{k}-E_{0})}|(W_{2}^{k})_{0,k}|^{2},

where
(W_{2}^{k})_{0,k}=|\langle E_{k},k|(W_{2}^{\dagger})|E_{0},0\rangle|
is the matrix element of
W_{2}^{k}
between the lowest energy states in the
e_{2}=k
and
e_{2}=0
sectors.
This matrix element should not scale with
T
, hence fitting to the exponential fall off determines
E_{k}-E_{0}
.

2.
A final remark regarding older work is that a
sketch
of the electric flux energies (given by our eqn. (
186
)) for pure
SU(6)
YM theory on a small
{\mathbb{T}}^{3}
as a function of
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
appears in Ch. 3, Fig. 3 of van Baal’s Ph.D. thesis
vanBaal:1984ra
(unpublished), where the figure is attributed to ’t Hooft.

Appendix A
Discrete symmetries

This appendix discusses the charge-conjugation and parity symmetries, which are essential concepts in
\cal{CP}
-violating processes.

A.1
Discrete symmetries in the two-component notation

The Euclidean action of the SYM
∗
theory (
12
) is rewritten for use below by taking the derivative act symmetrically:

\displaystyle S_{\scriptsize\mbox{SYM}^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{g^{2}}\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,_{\Box}\left[\frac{1}{2}F_{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}-\bar{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}(\partial_{\mu}\lambda_{\alpha}+i[A_{\mu},\lambda_{\alpha}])\right.

(189)

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad-\lambda^{\alpha}\sigma_{\mu\;\alpha\dot{\alpha}}(\partial_{\mu}\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}+i[A_{\mu},\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}])+m\lambda^{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}+m^{*}\bar{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}\right]\,.

Charge conjugation
maps the fundamental to its conjugate antifundamental representation of
SU(N)
, thus it acts as:
60
60

60

Here,
T^{a*}
is the complex conjugate of the
T^{a}
generator. It can equivalently be written as the transpose, recalling that we work with Hermitean generators.

\displaystyle{\cal{C}}:~~~A_{\mu}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle-A_{\mu}^{*}\equiv-A_{\mu}^{a}T^{a*},

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha}^{a}T^{a}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha}^{a}T^{a*},

(190)

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{a}T^{a}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{a}T^{a*}.

That this is a symmetry of (
189
), for real or complex
m
, follows from
\mathrm{tr}\,T^{a}T^{b}=\mathrm{tr}\,T^{a*}T^{a*}
and
[T^{a*},T^{b*}]=-if^{abc}T^{c*}
(while
[T^{a},T^{b}]=if^{abc}T^{c}
, where
f^{abc}
are the real structure constants). We notice that the gaugino bilinear
\mathrm{tr}\,\lambda\lambda
is invariant under
{\cal{C}}
.
Thus,
{\cal{C}}
is unbroken by
\langle\mathrm{tr}\,\lambda\lambda\rangle\neq 0
in SYM theory on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.

However, at finite volume,
the twisted boundary conditions break the
{\cal{C}}
symmetry, because the
{\cal{C}}
transformed field fails to obey the twisted boundary conditions, except in an
SU(2)
theory, where
{\cal{C}}
is part of the gauge group. The quickest way to see that the twists break
{\cal{C}}
is on the lattice: a plaquette
p
, twisted by a nontrivial two-form
{\mathbb{Z}}_{N}
gauge background
b_{p}=0,...,N-1
(implementing the twisted BC), contributes to the action a term proportional to
e^{i{2\pi\over N}b_{p}}\mathrm{tr}\,U_{p}+e^{-i{2\pi\over N}b_{p}}(\mathrm{tr}\,U_{p})^{*}
, where
\mathrm{tr}\,U_{p}
is the trace of the fundamental Wilson loop around
p
. As
{\cal{C}}
from (
A.1
) interchanges
U_{p}
and
U_{p}^{*}
, clearly, for
N\neq 2
(and, for even
N
,
b_{p}\neq{N\over 2}
) the action fails to be
{\cal{C}}
-invariant.

Thus, with nonzero twists,
{\cal{C}}
is only a symmetry in the infinite volume limit.

Parity
reverses the direction of three of the coordinates, which can be chosen at will in our Euclidean setup. In the
\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}=(-i\vec{\sigma},1)
basis we are using it is most straightforward to describe the action of parity on
\vec{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
. The choice of which three coordinates to reflect is a matter of convenience and a corresponding transform can be written for any choice, by changing the basis (in the end
{\cal{P}}
has the same action on the gaugino bilinear as given in (
193
), while the action on the components of the currents
\bar{\lambda}^{a}\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}\lambda^{b}
is appropriately modified from that given in (
193
)).

The action on the gauge field is:

\displaystyle{\cal{P}}:~~~A_{4}^{a}(\vec{x},x_{4})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle~~A_{4}^{a}(-\vec{x},x_{4}),

\displaystyle A^{a}_{i}(\vec{x},x_{4})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle-A^{a}_{i}(-\vec{x},x_{4}),~i=1,2,3.

(191)

On the spinors, the action of parity is as follows:
61
61

61

To avoid any confusion, the transformation should be read (omitting the spacetime argument and the group index) as:
\lambda_{1}\rightarrow i\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{1}}=i\bar{\lambda}_{\dot{2}}
,
\lambda_{2}\rightarrow i\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{2}}=-i\bar{\lambda}_{\dot{1}}
, etc.

\displaystyle{\cal{P}}:~\lambda^{a}_{\alpha}(\vec{x},x_{4})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle~~i\bar{\lambda}^{a\;\dot{\alpha}}(-\vec{x},x_{4}),

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}^{a}_{\dot{\alpha}}(\vec{x},x_{4})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle-i\lambda^{a\;\alpha}(-\vec{x},x_{4}).

(192)

While our discussion of
{\cal{P}}
can be considered self-contained, we note that the parity transform can be inferred by using the expressions for the states and spinor wave functions of a two-component Weyl spinor with Majorana mass from
Dreiner:2008tw
. One demands that parity map states
|\vec{p},s\rangle
, with spin (
s=\pm 1/2
) and momentum
\vec{p}
, to states
|\text{--}\vec{p},s\rangle
and analytically continues the resulting Minkowski field transformations to Euclidean signature, giving (
A.1
).
62
62

62

One can pursue this route to also find the time reversal transforms, demanding now that both momentum and angular momentum of single-particle states are reversed; we do not need to pursue this here.

From the above transformation rules, it is easy to see that the gaugino bilinears entering (
189
) transform as:

\displaystyle{\cal{P}}:~~~~\bar{\lambda}^{a}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\sigma}_{4}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\lambda^{b}_{\alpha}(\vec{x},x_{4})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle-\bar{\lambda}^{b}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\sigma}_{4}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\lambda^{a}_{\alpha}(-\vec{x},x_{4}),

(193)

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}^{a}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\lambda^{b}_{\alpha}(\vec{x},x_{4})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle~~\bar{\lambda}^{b}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\lambda^{a}_{\alpha}(-\vec{x},x_{4}),~i=1,2,3,

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\,\lambda^{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}(\vec{x},x_{4})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle~~\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}(-\vec{x},x_{4}).

Bilinears where
\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}
is replaced by
\sigma_{\mu}
transform analogously, due to
\bar{\lambda}\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}\lambda=-\lambda\sigma_{\mu}\bar{\lambda}
.

The form of the gaugino bilinear transformations (
193
), along with those of the gauge field (
A.1
), imply that the action density of eqn. (
189
) at
\vec{x}
is the same as the action density at
-\vec{x}
, provided
m=m^{*}
, hence
{\cal{P}}
is a symmetry for real
m
. In particular, in SYM theory on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, the gaugino bilinear expectation value in the
k
-th vacuum,
\langle\lambda\lambda\rangle\sim e^{i{2\pi\over N}k}
, breaks parity for
k\neq 0
(
P
is also unbroken in vacua with
k={N\over 2}
for even
N
).

In finite volume, in contrast to
{\cal{C}}
, not all twists on
{\mathbb{T}}^{4}
break
{\cal{P}}
. With the choice of
x_{i}
(
i=1,2,3
) as parity-reflected coordinates, twists
n_{ij}
in the spatial planes are consistent with the parity action: the infinite-volume
{\cal{P}}
-transforms of (
A.1
,
A.1
), require a modification at finite volume to ensure that the fields and their parity transforms obey identical spatial twisted boundary conditions. While the technical details are in
Cox:2021vsa
, heuristically this can be argued for by noting that spatial twists are “discrete magnetic flux” backgrounds and that magnetic fields preserve parity. Mixed space-time twists
n_{i4}
, however, violate
{\cal{P}}
. This is because in a Hamiltonian picture
n_{i4}
corresponds to inserting
\hat{T}_{i}^{n_{i4}}
in the partition function. Here,
\hat{T}_{i}
are the center symmetry generators in the
i
-th direction, which do not commute with
{\cal{P}}
: under parity,
\hat{T}_{i}\rightarrow\hat{T}_{i}^{-1}
(at
\theta=0
), or, equivalently, parity reverses the “discrete electric flux.”

A.2
Discrete symmetries in the four-component Majorana-spinor notation

We shall also use the
4
-component Majorana spinors
\Psi
and
\bar{\Psi}
. The Majorana spinors are handy when computing
{\cal{C}}{\cal{P}}
-odd correlators and are also more convenient to compare with lattice results, since they are used in lattice simulations of SYM, as in the recent
Butti:2022sgy
;
Bonanno:2024bqg
;
Bonanno:2024onr
.

The Majorana spinors are are defined in terms of the Weyl fermions
\lambda
and
\bar{\lambda}
, suppressing momentarily the color index (on which the transposition below does not act), as:
63
63

63

This is, essentially, the notation of
Butti:2022sgy
, see Sec. 2 there.

\displaystyle\Psi=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\lambda_{\alpha}\\
\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}\end{array}\right]\,,\quad\bar{\Psi}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\lambda^{\alpha}&\bar{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\lambda_{\beta}&\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\beta}}\end{array}\right]\cdot\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}-\epsilon^{\beta\alpha}&0\cr 0&-\epsilon_{\dot{\beta}\dot{\alpha}}\end{array}\right]\equiv\Psi^{t}\;C,~~C=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}-\epsilon^{\beta\alpha}&0\cr 0&-\epsilon_{\dot{\beta}\dot{\alpha}}\end{array}\right].

The use of the
4
-component spinor also necessitates the use of the Euclidean
\gamma
matrices which are given by:

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mu}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\sigma_{\mu}\\
\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}&0\end{array}\right]\,,\quad\gamma_{5}=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\gamma_{3}\gamma_{4}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}-I_{2\times 2}&0\\
0&I_{2\times 2}\end{array}\right]\,.

The fermionic terms in the action (
189
) take the form (with
\Psi=\Psi^{a}T^{a}
):

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{f}=-{1\over g^{2}}\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Psi}\gamma^{\mu}D_{\mu}\Psi+{m\over g^{2}}\;\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Psi}\;{1-\gamma_{5}\over 2}\;\Psi+{m^{*}\over g^{2}}\;\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Psi}\;{1+\gamma_{5}\over 2}\;\Psi

(209)

where we used

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Psi}\Psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\,\lambda\lambda+\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\lambda}\bar{\lambda}

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Psi}\gamma_{5}\Psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathrm{tr}\,\lambda\lambda+\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\lambda}\bar{\lambda}

(210)

For completeness, we now list the
{\cal{C}}
and
{\cal{P}}
transformations of the Majorana spinors (
A.2
), which follow from the two-component transformations listed above. We have that:

\displaystyle{\cal{C}}:~~~A_{\mu}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle-A_{\mu}^{*}\equiv-A_{\mu}^{a}T^{a*},

\displaystyle\Psi^{a}T^{a}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\Psi^{a}T^{a*},

(211)

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}^{a}T^{a}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}^{a}T^{a*},

while under parity (we give only the spinor transform; the gauge field transform is in (
A.1
)),

\displaystyle{\cal{P}}:~~~\Psi(\vec{x},x_{4})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle P\;\Psi(-\vec{x},x_{4})

(212)

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}(\vec{x},x_{4})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}(-\vec{x},x_{4})\;P^{\dagger},

where the definition of the matrix
P
and its properties are:

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&iI_{2\times 2}\\
iI_{2\times 2}&0\end{array}\right],\;P^{\dagger}P=1,~P^{2}=-1,

\displaystyle P^{\dagger}\gamma^{i}P

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\gamma^{i}\;\text{for}\;i=1,2,3,~P^{\dagger}\gamma^{4}P=\gamma^{4}.

(216)

{\cal{C}}
,
{\cal{P}}
, and the condensates:

Using the above relations under
{\cal P}
and
{\cal C}
, we may now check the transformation laws of the condensate
\bar{\Psi}\Psi
as well as the pseudo-scalar condensate
\bar{\Psi}\gamma_{5}\Psi
under the combined
{\cal C}{\cal P}
operation:
64
64

64

While we refer to the
\cal{CP}
transformation of (
217
,
218
), we recall that both the scalar and pseudoscalar condensates are, in fact,
{\cal{C}}
-invariant.

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}\Psi=\lambda^{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}+\bar{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}\xrightarrow{{\cal C}{\cal P}}\bar{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}+\lambda^{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}=\bar{\Psi}\Psi\,,

(217)

and

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}\gamma_{5}\Psi=-\lambda^{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}+\bar{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}\xrightarrow{{\cal C}{\cal P}}-\bar{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}+\lambda^{\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}=-\bar{\Psi}\gamma_{5}\Psi\,.

(218)

Thus, as expected,
\bar{\Psi}\Psi
behaves as a scalar, while
\bar{\Psi}\gamma_{5}\Psi
is a pseudo-scalar. A theory with
\langle\bar{\Psi}\gamma_{5}\Psi\rangle\neq 0
breaks
\cal{CP}
.

Appendix B
Small-
m
expansion of
Z^{T}
and
\langle\lambda\lambda\rangle
from the Hamiltonian

The Hamiltonian formalism is not well-suited to performing actual calculations, as renormalization and regularization are most easily done in a path-integral framework. However, the Hamiltonian formulation aids in the interpretation of various results, as we now discuss. We want to compute and interpret the leading—at small
mLN
—contribution to
Z^{T}
, as well as to the expectation values (
26
).
Taking into account the properties of
{\cal{H}}^{SYM}_{n_{34}=1}
described above, and using
\hat{H}_{m}=\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}}d^{3}x(-m(\hat{\lambda})^{2}-m^{*}(\hat{\lambda}^{\dagger})^{2})
, to leading and subleading order in
|m|
we find:
65
65

65

For
m=0
and without any operator insertions, the partition function
Z^{T}=1
, equaling
1/N
-the Witten index—because of supersymmetry, the contribution of
E>0
bosonic and fermionic states cancels, while the projection only takes the
e_{2}=0
ground state contribution.

\displaystyle Z^{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+{L_{1}m}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}}d^{3}x\;{1\over N}\sum\limits_{k,E,e_{2}}(-1)^{F}\langle E,e_{2}|(\hat{\lambda}(x))^{2}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}_{SYM}}\hat{T}_{2}^{k}|E,e_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle+{L_{1}m^{*}}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}}d^{3}x\;{1\over N}\sum\limits_{k,E,e_{2}}(-1)^{F}\langle E,e_{2}|(\hat{\lambda}(x)^{\dagger})^{2}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}_{SYM}}\hat{T}_{2}^{k}|E,e_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle+{L_{1}^{2}\over 2}m^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}}d^{3}xd^{3}y\;{1\over N}\sum\limits_{k,E,e_{2}}(-1)^{F}\langle E,e_{2}|(\hat{\lambda}(x))^{2}(\hat{\lambda}(y))^{2}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}_{SYM}}\hat{T}_{2}^{k}|E,e_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle+{L_{1}^{2}\over 2}(m^{*})^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}}d^{3}xd^{3}y\;{1\over N}\sum\limits_{k,E,e_{2}}(-1)^{F}\langle E,e_{2}|(\hat{\lambda}(x)^{\dagger})^{2}(\hat{\lambda}(y)^{\dagger})^{2}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}_{SYM}}\hat{T}_{2}^{k}|E,e_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle+{L_{1}^{2}\over 2}(mm^{*})\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}}d^{3}xd^{3}y\;{1\over N}\sum\limits_{k,E,e_{2}}(-1)^{F}\langle E,e_{2}|\left((\hat{\lambda}(x))^{2}(\hat{\lambda}(y)^{\dagger})^{2}+(\hat{\lambda}(x)^{\dagger})^{2}(\hat{\lambda}(y))^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\times e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}_{SYM}}\hat{T}_{2}^{k}|E,e_{2}\rangle+{\cal{O}}(|m|^{3})\,.

(219)

Clearly, a similar small-
|m|
expansion holds for the expectation values (
26
) and one only has to insert the appropriate
\hat{\cal{O}}
operator, see (
225
) below.

We now note that the chiral-center anomaly and the chiral transformation of
\lambda
of eqn. (
28
) imply relations between the matrix elements that appear above. These are valid for any set of
|E,e_{2}\rangle
degenerate states, so for brevity we only keep the
|e_{2}\rangle
label below. Thus, for any powers
2p
,
2q
of insertions of
\lambda
and
\bar{\lambda}
, we find

\displaystyle\langle e_{2}|\hat{X}^{-1}\;(\hat{X}\hat{\lambda}^{2p}(\hat{\lambda}^{\dagger})^{2q}\hat{X}^{-1})\;\hat{X}|e_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{i{2\pi\over N}(p-q)}\langle e_{2}-1|\lambda^{2p}(\lambda^{\dagger})^{2q}|e_{2}-1\rangle~\implies

\displaystyle~\langle e_{2}|\lambda^{2p}(\lambda^{\dagger})^{2q}\hat{|}e_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{i{2\pi\over N}(p-q)e_{2}}\langle e_{2}=0|\hat{\lambda}^{2p}(\hat{\lambda}^{\dagger})^{2q}|e_{2}=0\rangle~.

(220)

This further implies that, at every energy level, the sum over fluxes entering (
B
), using (
27
) becomes (here, the Kronecker delta in (
221
) equals unity if
k=q-p\;(\text{mod}N)
and zero otherwise):

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{e_{2}=0}^{N-1}\langle e_{2}|\hat{\lambda}^{2p}(\hat{\lambda}^{\dagger})^{2q}\hat{T}_{2}^{k}\hat{|}e_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle e_{2}=0|\hat{\lambda}^{2p}(\hat{\lambda}^{\dagger})^{2q}|e_{2}=0\rangle\;\sum_{e_{2}=0}^{N-1}e^{i{2\pi\over N}(p-q+k)e_{2}}

(221)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N\delta_{k,q-p(\text{mod}N)}\langle e_{2}=0|\hat{\lambda}^{2p}(\hat{\lambda}^{\dagger})^{2q}|e_{2}=0\rangle~.

Thus, the center-chiral anomaly implies a selection rule on the nonvanishing expectation values of operators like
\lambda^{2\ell}
(and c.c.) calculated from
(
B
).

Before we continue, let us make a comment. One might be tempted to also sum over
k
, as the sum
{1\over N}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N-1}
appears in every term in (
B
). Applying this extra sum to both sides of (
221
) leads to the result

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{k=0}^{N-1}N^{-1}\sum\limits_{e_{2}=0}^{N-1}\langle e_{2}|\hat{\lambda}^{2p}(\hat{\lambda}^{\dagger})^{2q}\hat{T}_{2}^{k}\hat{|}e_{2}\rangle=\langle e_{2}=0|\hat{\lambda}^{2p}(\hat{\lambda}^{\dagger})^{2q}|e_{2}=0\rangle,

(222)

thus ending up with a matrix element in the
e_{2}=0
state (as already stressed, due to the projector) and with no selection rule on
p,q
. While this is correct, we stress that the path integral representation of a matrix element
between fixed-
e_{2}
states involves a sum over distinct (fractional) topological sectors---as the two are discrete Fourier transforms of each other.
66
66

66

See the discussion near eqn. (
175
).
Since every topological sector has its own path integral representation, eqn. (
221
) for fixed
k
is, indeed, useful in the path integral framework and the semiclassical expansion.

Now, how do we calculate (
B
) and corresponding expectation values? We begin by taking the most conservative point of view that
mLN\ll 1
and
\Lambda LN\ll 1
—the small-
{\mathbb{T}}^{4}
limit
where semiclassics and the small-
m
expansion are certainly under control.
Thus, we replace the various Hilbert space traces appearing in the small-
m
expansion of
Z_{T}
(
B
), which all have the form,

\sum\limits_{E,e_{2}}(-1)^{F}\langle E,e_{2}|\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}}d^{3}x(\hat{\lambda})^{2p}(\hat{\lambda}^{\dagger})^{2q}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}}\hat{T}_{2}^{k}|E,e_{2}\rangle,

(223)

(or c.c.), with Euclidean path integrals on the small
{\mathbb{T}}^{4}
. The term (
223
) corresponds to a path integral with twists
n_{34}=1
and
n_{21}=k
.
In the small-
L\Lambda N
semiclassical limit, the leading contribution to each of the terms in the small-
m
expansion will come from the value of
k
allowed by the selection rule (
221
). The minimum amount of semiclassical suppression is then determined by noting that terms
\sim\hat{T}_{2}^{k}
are suppressed at least by
e^{-{8\pi^{2}\over g^{2}N}p}
, where
p={\rm{min}}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}|n-{k\over N}|
.

Thus, even without doing any calculation we can immediately argue that the leading semiclassical contributions to the order
m
and
m^{*}
sums in (
B
) come from
k=N-1
and
k=1
, respectively (which come with the minimal semiclassical suppression factor,
p=1
). On the other hand, the selection rule (
221
) implies that the order
m^{2}
and
(m^{*})^{2}
terms require
p=2
so we neglect those terms. The order
mm^{*}
term, however, allows
k=0
, which includes an unsuppressed perturbative contribution. Thus, we obtain:

\displaystyle Z^{T}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle(1+|m|^{2}L^{2}c_{0})+c\;(Lme^{-{8\pi^{2}\over Ng^{2}}}e^{i{\theta\over N}}+Lm^{*}{e^{-{8\pi^{2}\over Ng^{2}}}}e^{-i{\theta\over N}})+{\cal{O}}(|m|^{3},e^{-{16\pi^{2}\over Ng^{2}}})

(224)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+|m|^{2}L^{2}c_{0})+c^{\prime}L^{4}|m|\Lambda^{3}\;\cos{\theta+N\text{arg}\,m\over N}+{\cal{O}}(|m|^{3},e^{-{16\pi^{2}\over Ng^{2}}}),~

where on the last line we absorbed all undetermined factors in the coefficients
c_{0},c^{\prime}
. This result, including a calculation of
c_{0},c^{\prime}
will be obtained using path integrals, by a detailed small-
L_{\mu}
semiclassical calculation in the rest of the paper, see (
172
).
67
67

67

Note that
Z_{T}
is invariant under
U(1)_{spurious}
of eqn. (
1.4
).

Note that the order
m
terms in the partition function
Z_{T}
of (
224
) above do not allow an interpretation as a contribution to the ground state energy due to supersymmetry breaking. This is because in the small torus all energy states contribute to the Hilbert space traces. Later on, see section
6
, we consider the limit of large Euclidean time, which is, in principle, also semiclassical (yet is not analytically calculable) where such an extraction is possible.

We can also estimate the scaling of the expectation values of
\langle\hat{\lambda}^{2}\rangle
and
\langle(\hat{\lambda}^{\dagger})^{2}\rangle
, computed in the same
mLN\ll 1
limit. We simply insert the corresponding operator in each of the terms in the small-
m
expansion for
Z_{T}
in (
225
). Consider

\langle\hat{\lambda}^{2}\rangle
for definiteness, for which we find, for the numerator of (
26
) with
{\cal{O}}
replaced by
\hat{\lambda}^{2}(x_{1}=0)
, applying the selection rule (
221
) and indicating the value of
k
selected by an arrow in every term:

\displaystyle NZ_{T}\langle\hat{\lambda}^{2}(0)\rangle=\mathrm{tr}\,_{{\cal{H}}^{SYM}_{n_{34}=1}}\left((-1)^{F}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}_{SYM}}\;\hat{\lambda}^{2}(0)\;\hat{T}_{2}^{k}\right)\qquad\qquad{\leftarrow k=N-1\text{}}

\displaystyle+L_{1}m\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}_{x}}\sum\limits_{k,E,e_{2}}(-1)^{F}\langle E,e_{2}|(\hat{\lambda})^{2}(0)(\hat{\lambda}(x))^{2}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}_{SYM}}\hat{T}_{2}^{k}|E,e_{2}\rangle~~~~~{\leftarrow k=N-2,\text{ drop}}

\displaystyle+L_{1}m^{*}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}_{x}}\;\sum\limits_{k,E,e_{2}}(-1)^{F}\langle E,e_{2}|(\hat{\lambda})^{2}(0)(\hat{\lambda}(x)^{\dagger})^{2}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}_{SYM}}\hat{T}_{2}^{k}|E,e_{2}\rangle~~{\leftarrow k=0\text{ }}

\displaystyle+{L_{1}^{2}\over 2}m^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}_{x}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{3}_{y}}\;\sum\limits_{k,E,e_{2}}(-1)^{F}\langle E,e_{2}|(\hat{\lambda})^{2}(0)(\hat{\lambda}(x))^{2}(\hat{\lambda}(y))^{2}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}_{SYM}}\hat{T}_{2}^{k}|E,e_{2}\rangle~~{\leftarrow k=N-3,\text{ drop}}

\displaystyle+{L_{1}^{2}\over 2}(m^{*})^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}_{x}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{3}_{y}}\;\sum\limits_{k,E,e_{2}}(-1)^{F}\langle E,e_{2}|(\hat{\lambda})^{2}(0)(\hat{\lambda}(x)^{\dagger})^{2}(\hat{\lambda}(y)^{\dagger})^{2}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}_{SYM}}\hat{T}_{2}^{k}|E,e_{2}\rangle~~{\leftarrow k=1\text{ }}

\displaystyle+{L_{1}^{2}}(mm^{*})\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{3}_{x}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{3}_{y}}\;\sum\limits_{k,E,e_{2}}(-1)^{F}\langle E,e_{2}|(\hat{\lambda})^{2}(0)(\hat{\lambda}(x))^{2}(\hat{\lambda}(y)^{\dagger})^{2}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}_{SYM}}\hat{T}_{2}^{k}|E,e_{2}\rangle~~{\leftarrow k=N-1\text{}}

\displaystyle+{\cal{O}}(|m|^{3})~.

(225)

We now use the fact that

\mathrm{tr}\,_{{\cal{H}}^{SYM}_{n_{34}=1}}\left((-1)^{F}e^{-L_{1}\hat{H}_{SYM}}\;\hat{\lambda}^{2}(0)\;\hat{T}_{2}^{N-1}\right)\equiv N\langle\hat{\lambda}^{2}\rangle_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4},\;SYM}=N16\pi^{2}\Lambda^{3}

(226)

and proceed by keeping the lowest topological charge contribution in each case, recalling that
\sim\hat{T}_{2}^{k}
contributions
are suppressed by
e^{-{8\pi^{2}\over g^{2}N}p}
, where
p={\rm{min}}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}|n-{k\over N}|
.
To write the result, we introduce
new undetermined constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
:

\displaystyle Z_{T}\;\langle\hat{\lambda}^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 16\pi^{2}\Lambda^{3}(1+c_{1}|m|^{2}L^{2})+c_{2}\;{m^{*}\over L^{2}}+c_{3}\;(m^{*}L)^{2}~\Lambda^{3}e^{-i{\theta\over N}},

\displaystyle Z_{T}\;\langle(\hat{\lambda}^{\dagger})^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 16\pi^{2}\Lambda^{3}(1+c_{1}|m|^{2}L^{2})+c_{2}\;{m\over L^{2}}+c_{3}\;(mL)^{2}~\Lambda^{3}e^{i{\theta\over N}}~,

(227)

where in each case we kept the leading term in the semiclassical and small-
m
expansion only. The coefficient
c_{2}
is perturbative, proportional to
g^{2}
, while
c_{1},c_{3}
require a one-(fractional)-instanton calculation.

The expressions (
B
) for
Z_{T}\langle\hat{\lambda}^{2}\rangle
,
Z_{T}\langle(\hat{\lambda}^{\dagger})^{2}\rangle
obtained from the Hamiltonian formalism match the corresponding leading terms in (
5.1
) and (
5.1
) obtained by a path-integral semiclassical calculation, where a discussion of how
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
are determined can be found. Here, they were obtained using the small-
m
expansion, the selection rule following from the chiral-center anomaly in SYM, and the
\Lambda NL\ll 1
semiclassical power-counting.

Appendix C
The propagator in a general
Q={k\over N}
self-dual background

The goal of this Appendix is to compute the propagators in the fractional instanton background, needed in the computation of the correlation function (
3.2
). Typically, computing the propagator in the instanton background is a complex task. However,
as we discuss below, this task is made significantly simpler within the framework of the leading-order
\Delta
-expansion. Before providing the details, let us discuss the general idea behind calculating the propagators.

Our starting point is the fermion Lagrangian (
209
) written using the Majorana spinor. The corresponding equation of motion is

\displaystyle\left(-\not{D}+\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}mI_{2}&0\\
0&m^{*}I_{2}\end{array}\right]\right)\Psi=0\,,

and
\not{D}=\gamma_{\mu}D_{\mu}
.
In writing this equation, we stressed the
4\times 4
dimensional spinor space, while we have suppressed the
N\times N
indices of the internal space. This makes the treatment compact, while we shall discuss the internal spaces at the due time.
The corresponding eigenvalue problem—one needs to solve to find the Green’s function—is

\displaystyle-i\not{D}\Sigma_{n}=\omega_{n}\Sigma_{n}\,,

(231)

where
\not{D}=D_{\mu}\gamma_{\mu}
and
\Sigma_{n}
is a
4
-component spinor. We may also operate on the l.h.s of (
231
) with
\not{D}
to rewrite the eigenvalue problem in the form

\displaystyle\not{D}\not{D}\Sigma_{n}\equiv\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}D\bar{D}&0\cr 0&\bar{D}D\end{array}\right]\Sigma_{n}=-\omega_{n}^{2}\Sigma_{n}\,,\>\text{where}\;D\equiv\sigma_{\mu}D_{\mu},\bar{D}\equiv\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}D_{\mu}~.

Notice here that
-i\not{D}
is hermitian, and thus, the eigenvalues
\omega_{n}
are real. Further, for every mode with
\omega_{n}>0
, there exists a mode with eigenvalue
-\omega_{n}
, due to anticommutativity of
\not{D}
with
\gamma_{5}
.
68
68

68

Equivalently, in lattice gauge theory terminology, the spectrum of the antihermitean but
\gamma_{5}
-hermitean (
\not{D}^{\dagger}=\gamma_{5}\not{D}\gamma_{5}
) Dirac operator is on the imaginary axis and is symmetric upon reflection across the real axis.
The set of eigenmodes
\Sigma_{n}
—with the appropriate BCS—constitutes a complete set of states one uses to express the Green’s function.
We further note the well-known relations

\displaystyle D\bar{D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I_{2}D_{\mu}D_{\mu}+iF_{\mu\nu}\sigma^{\mu\nu},

\displaystyle\bar{D}D

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I_{2}D_{\mu}D_{\mu}~,

(235)

aka the “Weitzenböck formulae,” written here for a self-dual background
(\bar{\sigma}^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}=0
).

To construct the Green’s function, we now continue by considering fermions defined on general self-dual non-trivial background of topological charge
Q=k/N
.
We assume that the background is generic (as in our detuned
{\mathbb{T}}^{4}
with small
\Delta\neq 0
), such that the Dirac operator has
2k
zero modes in the undotted sector,
{1-\gamma_{5}\over 2}\Psi=\Psi
, i.e.
\lambda_{\alpha}
zero modes, and there are no zero modes in the
\bar{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}
sector—thus, as per the second line of (
C
), the spectrum of the adjoint Laplace operator is negative definite. For use below, we denote the
2k
undotted zero modes, obeying (
231
) with
\omega_{n}=0
, as
69
69

69

We stress that
p,\beta
are indices denoting the zero mode, while
\alpha
is the spinor index.

\displaystyle\Sigma_{p,\beta}^{(0)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\psi_{\alpha\,p,\beta}^{(0)}\\
0\end{array}\right],~p=1,...,k,~\beta=1,2,

using a composite index
(p,\beta)
to label the
2k
zero modes (this is motivated by their explicit expression on the detuned
{\mathbb{T}}^{4}
).

We let
\Sigma^{(+)}_{n}
and
\Sigma^{(-)}_{n}
be the positive and negative eigenmodes, with positive and negative eigenfrequencies,
\omega_{n}
and
-\omega_{n}
, respectively.
70
70

70

Starting from eqn. (
C
), we explicitly separate the positive and negative eigenvectors of
-i\not{D}
. Hence, from now on
\omega_{n}>0
.
Then, we have that
\Sigma_{n}^{(-)}
is expressed in terms of
\Sigma_{n}^{(+)}
:

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}^{(+)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\psi_{\alpha\,n}\\
\bar{\psi}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{n}\end{array}\right]\,,\quad\Sigma_{n}^{(-)}=\gamma_{5}\Sigma_{n}^{(+)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-\psi_{\alpha\,n}\\
\bar{\psi}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{n}\end{array}\right]\,,

The original eigenvalue equation
-i\not{D}\Sigma_{n}^{(+)}=\omega_{n}\Sigma_{n}^{(+)}
implies that we can write the nonzero modes undotted wave functions
\psi_{\alpha n}
in terms of the dotted ones
\bar{\psi}_{n}^{\dot{\alpha}}
:

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha n}=-\frac{i}{\omega_{n}}\sigma_{\mu\;\alpha\dot{\alpha}}D_{\mu}\bar{\psi}_{n}^{\dot{\alpha}}\equiv-\frac{i}{\omega_{n}}(D\psi_{n})_{\alpha}.

(244)

Further, as per eqns. (
C
,
C
),
\bar{\psi}_{n}^{\dot{\alpha}}
is an eigenvector of the adjoint covariant Laplace operator with eigenvalue
-\omega_{n}^{2}
. Thus, we
can write
\bar{\psi}_{n}^{\dot{\alpha}}
as the product of a two-component spinor
\bar{\zeta}^{(s)}_{\dot{\alpha}}
and an ordinary
\mathbb{C}
-function
\phi_{n}(x)
in the adjoint representation

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{n,s}^{\dot{\alpha}}(x)=\bar{\zeta}^{\dot{\alpha}(s)}\phi_{n}(x)\,,

(245)

where we explicitly add the index
s=1,2
to the index
n
, to account for the fact that there are two linearly independent choices of spinors
\bar{\zeta}^{\dot{\alpha}(s)}
.
Here,

\phi_{n}
is the eigenvector of the adjoint representation Laplace operator:

D_{\mu}D^{\mu}\phi_{n}=-\omega_{n}^{2}\phi_{n},\text{where},\forall n,~\omega_{n}^{2}>0.

(246)

As already stated, the adjoint representation Laplace operator is assumed to not have zero modes on the detuned
{\mathbb{T}}^{4}
. Thus, the functions
\phi_{n}
with nonzero
\omega_{n}^{2}
are a complete set of functions in the space of adjoint representation functions
\phi(x)
obeying the boundary conditions
\phi(x+L_{\mu})=\Omega_{\mu}(x)\phi(x)\Omega_{\mu}^{\dagger}(x)
. Without loss of generality, we shall impose a hermiticity condition
\phi^{\dagger}(x)=\phi(x)
, i.e. consider real adjoint fields in (
246
).
71
71

71

In fact, we are forced to consider Hermitean
\phi_{n}
obeying (
247
) and (
248
) when we consider the path integral formulation in terms of eigenmodes for
general
(i.e. not explicitly specified) self-dual backgrounds. This is because our action involves the fields
\bar{\Psi}
and
\Psi
, which are not hermitean conjugates of each other, but are related as in (
A.2
). See also Sec. B.2 in
Anber:2022qsz
for a similar treatment.

Thus, they obey the completeness relation

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{n}\varphi_{n}(x)\otimes\varphi_{n}(y)=\delta_{x,y}~~.

(247)

Here, we use
\delta_{x,y}
to denote the delta function in the space of adjoint scalar functions with the boundary conditions on the
{\mathbb{T}}^{4}
specified by
\Omega_{\mu}
; we stress that
\delta_{x,y}
carries also two sets of adjoint indices that we do not write explicitly.
72
72

72

It thus obeys, in view of (
247
,
248
), the delta-function relation
\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}dy\delta_{x,y}\phi(y)=\phi(x)
.

In addition, we normalize
\phi_{n}(x)
as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\phi_{n}(x)\phi_{m}(x)=\delta_{mn}\,.

(248)

The spinors
\bar{\zeta}^{\dot{\alpha}(s)}
, for
s=1,2
, are two constant independent spinors which we take explicitly as:

\displaystyle\bar{\zeta}^{\dot{\alpha}(s)}\equiv\delta^{\dot{\alpha}s},~\text{for}~s=1,2,\dot{\alpha}=1,2.

(249)

We now go back to our four component spinors
\Sigma_{n,s}^{(\pm)}
of eqn. (
C
), but with the extra index
s
added, as explained after eqn. (
245
) (recall that these spinors correspond to nonzero
-i\not{D}
eigenvalues
\pm\omega_{n}
, respectively), and express them in terms of the Laplacian eigenfunctions
\phi_{n}
, also recalling footnote
70
:

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n,s}^{(+)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}-\frac{i}{\omega_{n}}\sigma_{\mu\;\alpha s}D_{\mu}\phi_{n}\\
\delta^{\dot{\alpha}s}\phi_{n}\end{array}\right]\,,\quad\Sigma_{n,s}^{(-)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{i}{\omega_{n}}\sigma_{\mu\;\alpha s}D_{\mu}\phi_{n}\\
\delta^{\dot{\alpha}s}\phi_{n}\end{array}\right]\,,

Next, we the relation between the spinors
\Psi
and
\bar{\Psi}
of eqn. (
A.2
),
\bar{\Psi}=(\lambda_{\beta},\;\bar{\lambda}^{\dot{\beta}})\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\epsilon^{\beta\alpha}&0\cr 0&-\epsilon_{\dot{\beta}\dot{\alpha}}\end{array}\right)
, to define the functions
\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(\pm)}
:

\displaystyle\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(+)}=\left[\frac{i}{\omega_{n}}\sigma_{\mu\;\beta s}\epsilon^{\beta\alpha}D_{\mu}\phi_{n},\;\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}s}\phi_{n}\right]\,,\quad\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(-)}=\left[-\frac{i}{\omega_{n}}\sigma_{\mu\;\beta s}\epsilon^{\beta\alpha}D_{\mu}\phi_{n},\;\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}s}\phi_{n}\right]\,,

(255)

Likewise, from (
C
), we define the function
\bar{\Sigma}_{0,p}
:

\displaystyle\bar{\Sigma}^{(0)}_{p,\beta}=\left[-\psi^{(0)}_{\gamma p,\beta}\epsilon^{\gamma\alpha},0\right]=\left[\psi^{(0)\;\alpha}_{p,\beta},0\right],~p=1,...,k~.

(256)

In order to define the path integral, we expand
\Psi
and
\bar{\Psi}
in terms of the complete set of eigenfunctions of (
231
) (eqns. (
C
,
255
,
C
,
256
)):
73
73

73

To avoid confusion, we stress that the
SU(N)
group indices are carried by the Laplacian eigenvectors
\phi_{n}
, and therefore by the functions
\Sigma^{(0),(\pm)}
defined in terms of
\phi_{n}
. Thus, the equation for, e.g.
\Psi
, should really read:

\Psi_{ij}=\xi^{0}_{p,\beta}\Sigma^{(0)}_{p,\beta\;ij}+\xi_{n,s}^{+}\Sigma_{n,s\;ij}^{(+)}+\xi_{n,s}^{-}\Sigma_{n,s\;ij}^{(-)}

where
i,j=1,...N
are
SU(N)
indices. The Grassmann variables carry the indices numbering the Laplacian eigenvectors
\phi_{n}
and the functions
\Sigma_{n,s}
, i.e.
n
and
s=1,2
. (In the simplest case of a free Laplacian with periodic boundary conditions, as in section
D.1.1
, every element of the adjoint is an eigenvector of the Laplacian, hence the index
n
would then, in addition to the momentum label, also include the eigenvector label
ij
. For a general background, however, the eigenvector indices
n
are distinct from the
SU(N)
indices.) In what follows, we do not explicitly write the group indices in (
C
).

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\xi^{0}_{p,\beta}\Sigma^{(0)}_{p,\beta}+\xi_{n,s}^{+}\Sigma_{n,s}^{(+)}+\xi_{n,s}^{-}\Sigma_{n,s}^{(-)},~

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\xi^{0}_{p,\beta}\bar{\Sigma}^{(0)}_{p,\beta}+\xi_{n,s}^{+}\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(+)}+\xi_{n,s}^{-}\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(-)},

(257)

where
\xi_{p,\beta}
,
\xi_{n,s,\pm}
are Grassmann variables and a sum over
p=1,...,k
,
s=1,2
, and
n
is assumed in each line.

Before we continue we note various useful identities. First, we note that:

\displaystyle\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Sigma}^{(0)}_{p,\beta}\Sigma_{n,s}^{(\pm)}\sim\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\psi_{p,\beta}^{(0)\;\alpha}\sigma_{\mu\;\alpha s}D_{\mu}\phi_{n}=0,~~\text{likewise}~\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(\pm)}\Sigma^{(0)}_{p}=0,

(258)

because
\psi_{p,\beta}^{(0)\;\alpha}
and
\sigma_{\mu\;\alpha s}D_{\mu}\phi_{n}
are eigenfunctions of
D\bar{D}
with different eigenvalues (the vanishing also follows by direct integration by parts).
We normalize the zero-modes (
C
) as follows:
74
74

74

The zero modes obeying (
259
) are given, to order
\Delta^{0}
, in eqn. (
80
).

\displaystyle\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Sigma}^{(0)}_{p,\beta}\Sigma^{(0)}_{q,\beta^{\prime}}=\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\psi^{(0)\;\alpha}_{p,\beta}\psi^{(0)}_{q,\beta^{\prime},\alpha}=\delta_{pq}\epsilon_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}~,

(259)

recalling that
p,q=1,...,k
and
\beta,\beta^{\prime}=1,2
.

Next, we consider the
\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(+)}\Sigma_{m,s^{\prime}}^{(-)}
inner product. Rearranging the
\sigma
-matrices, and integrating by parts, we find, from (
C
,
255
):

\displaystyle\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(+)}\Sigma_{m,s^{\prime}}^{(-)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\epsilon_{s^{\prime}\dot{\gamma}}{(\bar{\sigma}_{\nu}\sigma_{\mu})^{\dot{\gamma}}_{\;\;s}\over\omega_{n}\omega_{m}}\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\phi_{n}D_{\mu}D_{\nu}\phi_{m}+\epsilon_{s^{\prime}s}\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\phi_{n}\phi_{m}~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}0~.

(260)

To show its vanishing, we note that in view of (
248
), the integral in the second term is
\delta_{nm}
.
The first term is
(\bar{\sigma}_{\nu}\sigma_{\mu})^{\dot{\gamma}}_{\;\;s}\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\phi_{n}D_{\mu}D_{\nu}\phi_{m}=\delta^{\dot{\gamma}}_{\;\;s}\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\phi_{n}D_{\mu}D_{\mu}\phi_{m}+(\bar{\sigma}_{\nu\mu})^{\dot{\gamma}}_{\;\;s}\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\phi_{n}[D_{\mu},D_{\nu}]\phi_{m}=-\delta^{\dot{\gamma}}_{s}\omega_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}
, where we used the self-duality of the background,
\bar{\sigma}_{\nu\mu}F_{\nu\mu}=0
, the Laplace equation (
246
), and orthonormality of the
\phi_{n}
basis (
248
). Plugging this back in (
260
) shows that, indeed
\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(+)}\Sigma_{m,s^{\prime}}^{(-)}=0
.

The vanishing of the inner product
\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(-)}\Sigma_{m,s^{\prime}}^{(+)}
follows by literally repeating the same steps, thus

\displaystyle\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(-)}\Sigma_{m,s^{\prime}}^{(+)}=0~.

(261)

We are left to determine

\displaystyle\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(+)}\Sigma_{m,s^{\prime}}^{(+)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\epsilon_{s^{\prime}\dot{\gamma}}{(\bar{\sigma}_{\nu}\sigma_{\mu})^{\dot{\gamma}}_{\;\;s}\over\omega_{n}\omega_{m}}\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\phi_{n}D_{\mu}D_{\nu}\phi_{m}+\epsilon_{s^{\prime}s}\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\phi_{n}\phi_{m}.

(262)

We note that (
262
) only differs from (
260
) by the sign of the first term. Thus, instead of cancelling, the two contributions add up and we obtain:

\displaystyle\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(+)}\Sigma_{m,s^{\prime}}^{(+)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\epsilon_{s^{\prime}s}\delta_{nm}.

(263)

Likewise, we find:

\displaystyle\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(-)}\Sigma_{m,s^{\prime}}^{(-)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\epsilon_{s^{\prime}s}\delta_{nm}.

(264)

To calculate the action for
\Im m\neq 0
, we shall also need the various integrals with
\gamma_{5}
inserted. As
\gamma_{5}
simply changes the sign of the first term appearing in (
260
) and (
262
), it is easy to see that the results are as follows

\displaystyle\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Sigma}^{(0)}_{p,\beta}\gamma_{5}\Sigma^{(0)}_{q,\beta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\delta_{pq}\epsilon_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}~,

\displaystyle\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(-)}\gamma_{5}\Sigma_{m,s^{\prime}}^{(-)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,~\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(+)}\gamma_{5}\Sigma_{m,s^{\prime}}^{(+)}=0,

(265)

\displaystyle\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(+)}\gamma_{5}\Sigma_{m,s^{\prime}}^{(-)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Sigma}_{n,s}^{(-)}\gamma_{5}\Sigma_{m,s^{\prime}}^{(+)}=2\delta_{n,m}\epsilon_{s^{\prime}s}~,

which also follow from (
260
,
261
,
264
) by recalling
\gamma_{5}\Sigma^{(-)}=\Sigma^{(+)}
and
\gamma_{5}\Sigma^{(+)}=\Sigma^{(-)}
.

Armed with (
258
,
260
,
261
,
263
,
264
,
C
) we substitute (
C
), use (
231
),
and obtain the fermion action (
209
) as a bilinear in the Grassmann variables, with a sum over all repeated indices understood:

\displaystyle g^{2}\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}{\cal{L}}_{f}=\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}\left(-\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Psi}\not{D}\Psi+\Re m\;\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Psi}\Psi-i\Im m\;\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{\Psi}\gamma_{5}\Psi\right)

(266)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\xi^{0}_{p\beta}\xi^{0}_{p\gamma}\epsilon_{\beta\gamma}m+\xi_{n,s}^{+}\xi_{n,s^{\prime}}^{+}\epsilon_{ss^{\prime}}2\left[i\omega_{n}-\Re m\right]-\xi_{n,s}^{-}\xi_{n,s^{\prime}}^{-}\epsilon_{ss^{\prime}}2\left[i\omega_{n}+\Re m\right]+(\xi_{n,s}^{+}\xi_{n,s^{\prime}}^{-}+\xi_{n,s}^{-}\xi_{n,s^{\prime}}^{+})\epsilon_{ss^{\prime}}\left[2i\Im m\right]~.

This can be rewritten in a manner convenient to explicitly do the Grassmann integrals with
e^{-S_{f}}=e^{-\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}{\cal{L}}_{f}}
:

\displaystyle-\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}{\cal{L}}_{f}

(267)

\displaystyle=\xi^{0}_{p1}\xi^{0}_{p2}{2m\over g^{2}}-\xi_{n,1}^{+}\xi_{n,2}^{+}{4\over g^{2}}\left[-i\omega_{n}+\Re m\right]-\xi_{n,1}^{-}\xi_{n,2}^{-}{4\over g^{2}}\left[i\omega_{n}+\Re m\right]+(\xi_{n,1}^{+}\xi_{n,2}^{-}+\xi_{n,1}^{-}\xi_{n,2}^{+})\left[{4\over g^{2}}i\Im m\right],

with summations over
p=1,...,k
and
n
, the eigenmodes of the Laplacian (
246
) understood.

To keep track of any sign ambiguity, we define the path integral measure without yet explicitly specifying the order of integration over the Grassmann variables:

\displaystyle d\Psi\equiv\prod\limits_{p=1}^{k}d^{2}\xi^{0}_{p}\;\prod_{n}d^{4}\xi_{n},

(268)

where

represents the measure of integration over

and
\xi^{0}_{p,2}
and

—over
\xi_{n,1(2)}^{\pm}
.
This already allows us to calculate the unregulated fermion determinant, which we label
{\cal{D}}_{k}^{f}(m)

\displaystyle{\cal{D}}_{k}^{f}(m)

(269)

\displaystyle\equiv\int d\Psi\;e^{-\int\limits_{{\mathbb{T}}^{4}}{\cal{L}}_{f}}=\left({2m\over g^{2}}\right)^{k}\prod_{n}\left({16\over g^{4}}(\omega_{n}^{2}+|m|^{2})\right)\prod_{p=1}^{k}\underbrace{\int d^{2}\xi^{0}_{p}\xi^{0}_{p1}\xi^{0}_{p2}}_{{\epsilon}_{p}}\prod_{n}\underbrace{\int d^{4}\xi_{n}\xi_{n,1}^{+}\xi_{n,2}^{+}\xi_{n,1}^{-}\xi_{n,2}^{-}}_{{\epsilon}_{n}},

where we define
{\epsilon}_{p}
and
{\epsilon}_{n}

, depending on our definition of

\displaystyle{\cal{D}}_{k}^{f}(m)=\left({2m\over g^{2}}\right)^{k}\prod_{n}\left({16\over g^{4}}(\omega_{n}^{2}+|m|^{2})\right)\prod_{p=1}^{k}{\epsilon}_{p}\prod_{n}{{\epsilon}_{n}}.

(271)

The fermion two-point function

\displaystyle\langle\Psi(x)\otimes\bar{\Psi}(y)\rangle_{unnorm.}

\displaystyle=\langle(\xi^{0}_{p,\beta}\Sigma^{(0)}_{p,\beta}(x)+\xi_{n,s}^{+}\Sigma_{n,s}^{(+)}(x)+\xi_{n,s}^{-}\Sigma_{n,s}^{(-)}(x))\otimes(\xi^{0}_{q,\beta^{\prime}}\bar{\Sigma}^{(0)}_{q,\beta^{\prime}}(y)+\xi_{n^{\prime},s^{\prime}}^{+}\bar{\Sigma}_{n^{\prime},s^{\prime}}^{(+)}(y)+\xi_{n^{\prime},s^{\prime}}^{-}\bar{\Sigma}_{n^{\prime},s^{\prime}}^{(-)}(y))\rangle.

\displaystyle\;+\langle\xi_{n,1}^{+}\xi_{n,2}^{+}\rangle\left[\Sigma_{n,1}^{+}(x)\otimes\bar{\Sigma}_{n,2}^{+}(y)-\Sigma_{n,2}^{+}(x)\otimes\bar{\Sigma}_{n,1}^{+}(y)\right]+\langle\xi_{n,1}^{-}\xi_{n,2}^{-}\rangle\left[\Sigma_{n,1}^{-}(x)\otimes\bar{\Sigma}_{n,2}^{-}(y)-\Sigma_{n,2}^{-}(x)\otimes\bar{\Sigma}_{n,1}^{-}(y)\right]

Next, from (

75
75

75

To avoid confusion about the dimensionality of the propagator, recall that our functions

have mass dimension

, as per e.g. (

\displaystyle\langle\xi^{0}_{p,1}\xi^{0}_{q,2}\rangle={\cal{D}}_{k}^{f}(m)\delta_{pq}{g^{2}\over 2}{m^{*}\over|m|^{2}},

For the nonzero modes, we find, similarly, that for the same-sign correlators the
